## Document 028: 20261015【投研報告】市場快訊_通膨升溫壓抑美股表現，近期聚焦財報結果

Report source: Yuanta (市場評論)
Date: 2026-10-15
Pages: 4
Extraction status: ok

通膨升溫壓抑美股表現，近期聚焦財報結果
2026 年 10 月 15 日
一、 市場動態
1. 科技股領跌，費半連 6 日收黑：美股主要指數 10 月 14 日全數下跌，道瓊工業指數下
跌 0.09%，收 51,621.48 點；S&P 500 指數下跌 1.11%，收 7,718.94 點，為 7 月 29
日以來最大單日跌幅；Nasdaq 綜合指數下跌 1.88%，收 26,692.87 點，為 7 月 23
日以來最大單日跌幅。費城半導體指數下跌 4.30%，收 11,684.38 點，連續第６個交
易日走低，為 2025 年 5 月以來最長連跌紀錄。

2. 類股非全面下挫，資金仍呈輪動現象：半導體及 AI 相關股票在 10/14 跌幅尤其明顯
，AMD 下跌 5.73%、Nvidia 下跌 3.16%、Broadcom 下跌 3.10%，AI 基礎建設相關
的 CoreWeave、Nebius、Oracle 及 Dell 跌幅更深，顯示市場對資本支出規模較大、
融資成本敏感度較高的 AI 相關企業風險偏好明顯下降。不過，S&P 500 的 11 大類
股中有 8 類下跌，資訊科技及通訊服務為主要拖累來源，但醫療保健、必需消費與能
源類股仍逆勢上漲。
3. 美國 10 年期公債殖利率盤中急升後回落：美國 10 年期公債殖利率 10/14 由 5.2%左
右快速升至約 5.33%，逼近近期高點；然而，隨後市場消化 Fed 官員較審慎的政策訊
號，殖利率自高檔回落，終場約收於 5.19%，較前一交易日 5.21%小幅下降約 2 個基
點。
二、 下跌原因分析
1. 美 9 月通膨高於預期 ，Fed 再升息風險重新升高 ：美國 10/14 公布 9 月 CPI 月增 0.6%

、年增率也由 8 月的 3.4%上揚至 3.7%；核心 CPI 月增 0.3%、年增率由 8 月的 2.4%
升至 2.6%。其中汽油價格單月大漲 9.1%，貢獻整體 CPI 月增幅超過一半；住宅成本
亦月增 0.4%，顯示物價壓力仍具黏性。此數據公布後，市場重新提高 Fed 進一步升
息的預期 ，CME FedWatch 所反映的 10 月升息 1 碼機率一度由前一日約 22%提高至
37%。雖然截至 10/15 台北時間早上 7 點左右該機率回降至 29.3%，這也提醒投資人
Fed 的升息路徑仍存在不確定性。
2. 高利率同時壓縮科技股估值與提高 AI 融資成本 ：科技及半導體公司未來獲利成長占評
價的比重較高，因此對折現率變化相對敏感。當長天期美債殖利率維持 5%以上，市
場願意支付的本益比自然受到限制，即使企業獲利仍維持成長，股價也可能因評價倍
數下降而出現修正。另一方面，本輪 AI 投資需要大量資料中心、伺服器、電力與網路
基礎設施，高利率亦直接增加企業融資成本，反映信用市場對高度依賴外部資金的 AI

基礎建設公司要求更高風險溢酬。

確性不做任何擔保，如有錯漏或疏忽，本行及本行之任何董事或受僱人，毋須負任何法律責任。任何人因信賴此等資料而做出或改變投資決
策，應審慎衡量本身之需求及投資風險，自行承擔一切投資風險並自負盈虧，本行不負任何法律責任。本報告受中華民國著作權法保護為元
大銀行所有，非經本行同意，不得將本報告內容之一部或全部加以複製、轉載或散布。本報告所載述的意見本行可隨時予以更改或撤回，恕
不另行通知。
3. AI 投資焦點由「支出規模」轉向「投資報酬率」:近期市場對 AI 的疑慮逐漸由「需求
是否成長」 ，轉向「龐大的資本支出能否轉化為足夠營收、獲利與自由現金流」 。
Broadcom 執行長陳福陽近期亦指出 ，AI 未來發展的重要限制可能逐漸由晶片或記憶
體供應轉向資本本身。我們認為，這並不代表AI 資本支出週期即將結束，而是市場已
由第一階段的 「資本支出愈大、成長想像愈高」 ，逐步進入更重視資本效率與投資報酬
率的第二階段。未來財務結構、現金流與AI 商業化能力，將成為不同企業評價分化的
重要因素。
4. 中國記憶體競爭疑慮加重半導體短線壓力：中國長鑫存儲（CXMT）10/10 傳出記憶

體技術取得進展 ， 引發市場對未來DRAM 供給增加及價格競爭的疑慮 ， 記憶體相關類
股率先承壓，並進一步影響整體半導體市場情緒。惟 DRAM 與 GPU、客製化 ASIC、
先進晶圓製程及先進封裝的產業競爭與需求結構並不相同。我們認為，儘管記憶體競
爭升溫值得關注，但目前尚不足以延伸為整體 AI 半導體需求同步轉弱。
5. 高檔籌碼加速獲利了結：AI 與半導體仍是 2026 年美股表現最強勢的投資主題之一，
部分科技股今年累計漲幅已相當可觀。當通膨、殖利率及AI 投資報酬疑慮同時升高，
先前漲幅較大、投資人部位較集中的族群自然成為優先調節對象。因此，本波費半快
速修正除了總經與基本面疑慮外，亦包含籌碼降溫及風險部位收縮因素，使短期價格
波動幅度可能高於企業基本面實際變化。
三、市場看法
1. 類股輪動明顯，尚非全面看空美股：科技與 AI 相關股票 10 月 14 日明顯承壓，但醫
療保健、必需消費與能源類股仍逆勢上漲，道瓊工業指數僅下跌 0.09%。此外，S&P
500 成分股下跌家數與上漲家數之比約為 1.1：1；相較於指數下跌 1.11%，市場廣度

並未同步大幅惡化。這反映資金主要由高評價且前期漲幅較大的科技與AI 族群，轉向
現金流較穩定的防禦型產業。現階段市場特徵較接近類股輪動與風格切換，而非無差
別的全面風險撤離，亦尚未看到美股企業獲利或經濟基本面全面反轉的明確訊號。
2. 美股科技股財報前風險降載，Q3 仍存在重新定價空間：近期科技股修正，費城半導
體指數連續６個交易日下跌，也可能部分反映第三季財報季正式展開前，投資人在股
價先前漲幅較大、評價偏高及利率環境不確定下，提前降低風險曝險。真正決定後續
市場方向的關鍵 ， 仍在於實際企業獲利能否支持目前的高成長預期 。根據 Bloomberg
季度獲利成長追蹤資料，今年 Q1 與 Q2 財報季期間的 EPS 成長率經驗顯示，隨實際
財報陸續公布，企業獲利成長曾明顯優於財報季開始前的市場預期。儘管這並不保證
Q3 將複製前兩季的上修幅度，但目前市場因財報不確定性所反映的風險折價仍存在
被實際財報重新驗證的可能。若大型科技及半導體公司後續營收、獲利與AI 需求指引
實際公布結果優於預期，近期風險溢價可望部分收斂；反之，若獲利成長無法跟上目

前評價，市場整理時間則可能進一步延長。

不另行通知。
3. 費半修正幅度大，但 AI 需求尚未出現全面反轉：ASML 在 10/14 所公布的最新第三
季財報反而顯示 AI 相關設備需求仍相當強勁，該公司表示 2027 與 2028 年規劃產能
可能仍無法完全滿足客戶需求，部分客戶亦已表達對 2029 及 2030 年設備需求的興
趣。儘管費城半導體指數近期市場情緒轉弱，但由 ASML 的財報表現來看，目前仍未
看到大型 AI 客戶全面取消晶片訂單、主要雲端服務商同步削減 AI 資本支出，或先進
製程需求快速降溫等結構性訊號。
4. 台積電營收顯示 AI 需求暢旺 ， 留意10/15 法說會內容：台積電 10/8 公布的第三季營

收達新台幣 1.49 兆元、年增約 50%，創歷史新高，並高於市場原先預估的 1.46 兆元
；9 月單月營收年增 54.6%。強勁的營收表現仍反映 AI 與先進製程需求具有支撐。目
前市場因此呈現「企業營收仍快速成長，但股票評價下降」的狀況。高殖利率環境下
，EPS 上升與本益比下降可以同時發生，後續股價方向將取決於企業獲利上修幅度是
否足以抵銷評價收縮。該公司將於 10/15 下午舉行第三季法人說明會。市場重點將集
中於第四季展望、AI 與 HPC 需求、先進製程、先進封裝及未來資本支出規劃。公司
對 AI 需求與產能投資的最新判斷，將成為判斷近期費半修正主要屬於評價調整，或產
業基本面是否開始出現變化的重要依據。
5. AI 與半導體進入基本面分化階段：我們認為，在高利率環境下，高度仰賴外部融資、
自由現金流尚未成熟的 AI 基礎建設業者 ， 受到的資金成本壓力明顯高於具穩定現金流
的大型科技與雲端服務商。同樣地，記憶體、GPU、ASIC、先進製程及先進封裝各有
不同供需結構。我們預期下一階段 AI 行情將更加重視訂單能見度、產品競爭力、毛利

率、自由現金流及 AI 營收實際兌現，產業內部強弱分化程度可能進一步提高。
6. Fed 路徑仍存不確定性，惟企業獲利可望提供股市支撐：美國 9 月 CPI 升溫使市場重
新評估 Fed 後續升息時點與幅度，美國利率政策仍存在不確定性，長天期美債殖利率
也可能維持高檔。不過，現階段企業獲利成長與AI 相關需求仍具韌性，若第三季財報

策，應審慎衡量本身之需求及投資風險，自行承擔一切投資風險並自負盈虧，本行不負任何法律責任。本報告受中華民國著作權法保護為元
大銀行所有，非經本行同意，不得將本報告內容之一部或全部加以複製、轉載或散布。本報告所載述的意見本行可隨時予以更改或撤回，恕
不另行通知。
延續前兩季優於預期的趨勢，企業 EPS 若能上修有望部分抵銷高利率對本益比的壓縮
效果。換言之，後續市場表現未必只由 Fed 政策主導，企業實際獲利能否持續兌現，
將是支撐科技與半導體股中期走勢的重要力量。
四、投資建議
1. 短線聚焦美股財報與 Fed 決策，科技股中期趨勢料不致有變：近期市場同時面臨財報
前的觀望態勢、美債殖利率偏高及 AI 資本支出疑慮等不確定因素，短線風險偏好可能
受到壓抑，後續宜關注 10 月美股財報對企業獲利與資本支出展望，以及 FOMC 會議
所釋出的政策訊號。中期而言，目前AI 相關企業營運與資本支出尚未出現全面轉弱跡

象，科技產業基本面仍具一定支撐，但在利率維持高檔及市場評價偏高的環境下，後
續宜更加重視企業獲利、現金流及資本支出的實際變化。
2. 中天期債券風險報酬仍較均衡 ：美國 9 月 CPI 升溫使短端殖利率持續受到 Fed 政策路
徑影響，而 20～30 年超長端仍面臨財政赤字、國債供給與期限溢價偏高等結構性壓
力。相較之下，目前殖利率已位於歷史相對高檔，若後續核心通膨或能源價格改善，
中段殖利率具有回落條件，同時其存續期間風險又低於超長端，因此現階段以中天期
美國公債及投資級債的風險報酬結構相對較佳。

市場焦點
美國
公債回購
• 美國財政部9月9日宣布，將於翌日回購最高60億美元的10至20年期美債，低於部分市場期待
的80億至100億美元規模 。民間持有美債規模達 31.8兆美元，相較之下 ，本次60億美元回購
僅約占民間持債規模 0.02%，對整體影響有限 。此外，約6成的美債期限不足 5年，意味未來
再融資需求仍高，若財政赤字未明顯收斂，長端殖利率上行壓力仍存。
日本央行
• 日本第二季GDP年化季增率上修至 1.4%(優於初值1.1%)，儘管伊朗戰爭引發原油供應緊張給
經濟帶來壓力，但GDP增速依舊高於0.7%潛在經濟成長率，雖然經濟表現支持日本央行(BOJ)
逐步實施貨幣政策正常化 ，但更關鍵的催化劑來自日美兩國協調干預匯市 ，遏制日圓極端貶
值的政治壓力，彭博經濟研究預期日本央行將在9月會議上加息25個基點。

美國
通膨
• 美國8月生產者物價指數 （PPI）年增5.4%，略高市場預期的 5.3%，核心PPI年增4.6%，符合
預期。8月消費者物價指數（CPI）年增3.35%、核心CPI年增2.4%，均符合市場預期，但核心
CPI月升幅0.3%，高於市場預期的 0.2%。數據公佈後，FedWatch顯示市場預期9月升息機率，
從前一周的50%攀升至86%。

美國8月通膨數據(CPI)
• 美國8月通膨數據：8月美國CPI月增0.4%、年增3.4%，核心CPI月增0.3%，核心服務通膨升溫至0.5%，顯示通膨降溫仍不
均衡，能源價格上漲為主要推手，市場預估8月核心PCE月增約0.3%、年增約3.8%，仍高於Fed 2%目標，搭配強勁就業數
據，9月升息1碼的壓力升高。不過，本次通膨主要來自能源、需求及AI投資帶動，與2021～2022年供給型通膨有所不同。
• 市場反應與展望：CPI公布後，2年期美債殖利率一度上升，惟長天期殖利率反而走低，顯示市場關注焦點並非單純升息，而
是Fed能否維持抗通膨政策可信度。整體而言，升息未必等同Risk-off，若經濟與企業獲利維持韌性，股市仍具支撐。
資料來源： investing.com，2026.9.14

能源成本回升推升PPI，通膨壓力牽動Fed政策空間
• 能源成本回升，PPI重新升溫：美國8月PPI月增0.4%、年增5.4%，增幅均明顯高於7月，主要受到商品價格上漲帶動；其
中最終需求商品價格月增1.1%，能源價格上升4.2%，柴油更是大漲逾2成，成為商品價格上升的重要來源，惟最終需求服
務價格僅略增0.1%，顯示本次PPI升溫仍以能源及商品價格為主。
• 成本壓力仍在，利率政策不確定性升高： 扣除食品、能源及貿易服務後的核心 PPI月增0.3%，微低於7月的0.4%，但年增
4.7%，顯示價格壓力仍未完全消退；其中運輸與倉儲服務價格上升2.3%，反映部分成本仍具黏著性，致使市場對Fed利率
政策的重新定價明顯升溫，進而推動股、債市短線波動擴大。
• 市場觀察：由於本次PPI升溫主要來自能源成本 ，但服務價格壓力整體仍相對溫和 ，因此尚不足以視為通膨全面升溫 ，然

能源價格若持續走高 ，將提高Fed維持較高利率的壓力 ，建議投資人短線宜降低長天期債券與高估值成長股的追價 ，債券
可優先配置短中天期、高品質債券，股票則可關注能源、金融及具定價能力企業，提高投組對利率與成本波動的承受度。
資料來源：U.S. Bureau of Labor Statistics，Producer Price Indexes – Aug,  2026，2026.9.11

• 財政部對美債的回購規模不如預期：美國財政部9月9日宣布，將於翌日回購最高60億美元的10至20年期美債，主要鎖定流

動性較低的舊券，以改善長天期債券市場流動性。雖然本次回購高於先前設定的每次至少40億美元，但低於部分市場期待的
80億至100億美元規模。消息公布後10年期公債殖利率逼近5%，30年期殖利率亦突破5.3%。
• 回購規模在高額赤字背景下，難以有效拉低長天期公債殖利率：美國今年公債發行規模較2025年增加11.8%，民間持有美債
規模達31.8兆美元、年增8.2%，相較之下，本次60億美元回購僅約占民間持債規模0.02%，對整體影響有限。此外，約6成
的美債期限不足5年，意味未來再融資需求仍高，若財政赤字未明顯收斂，長端殖利率上行壓力仍存。反觀新興國家基本面
持續改善，且當地債市有貨幣潛在升值空間，可作為全球債券配置的分散來源。

• 地緣政治動盪推升油價，但全球經濟依然展現韌性：美國和伊朗的衝突已經進入第7個月，能源價格上升及地緣政治風險仍
對全球經濟與市場信心形成壓力，但AI投資熱潮持續推升科技需求，使全球經濟展現優於預期的韌性。其中，亞洲仍是全球
成長動能最強的區域之一，2026年亞洲（不含日本）經濟成長率預估達4.7%，明顯高於全球平均2.9%及成熟市場1.6%。在
內需與科技出口支撐下，新興亞洲基本面相對穩健，具備較佳的中長期配置條件。

• AI資本支出延續，台韓成為核心受惠市場：AI資本支出持續擴張，2026年全球雲端AI資本支出預估達7,960億美元，並帶動
AI半導體市場至2030年突破7,500億美元。亞洲掌握全球半導體供應鏈核心，台灣受惠於先進製程與先進封裝需求，韓國則
受惠於HBM記憶體需求，台韓可望成為AI基礎建設擴張的主要受惠市場；同時，中國與印度則提供較穩定的內需及經濟成
長支撐。2026年亞洲企業盈餘成長率預估達72.13%，顯著高於全球平均，建議新興亞洲以台韓科技股為主要成長配置，並
搭配中國、印度市場分散區域風險。

短債供給增加未引發融資緊張，短天期債券防禦價值提升
• 回購市場維持穩定，短債供給暫未造成壓力：美國回購市場今年以來維持平穩，儘管9月短債償付與企業繳稅可能帶來短
暫資金波動，但SOFR與IORB利差仍處相對正常區間 ，顯示目前準備金與短期流動性尚稱充裕 。此外SOFR/IORB差值對
短期美債淨發行增加的敏感度明顯低於2025年底，反映流動性環境偏寬鬆。
• 貨幣基金成長放緩，供給吸收能力受到限制：零售資金流入偏弱，使貨幣市場基金資產增速落後 ，2026年迄今僅增加約
325億美元，遠低於2025年同期的2,050億美元；即便Q4季節性回升，新增資金對短債供給的承接能力仍可能有限。
• 後市觀察：年底前短債淨發行規模預估約3,250億美元，後續需關注短債供給、企業繳稅及財政部長端公債回購對資金面

的影響。建議投資人配置上可提高 1～3年期美國國庫券或短天期美債 ETF比重，以取得票息收益並降低長端殖利率波動
對債券價格的影響。
資料來源：US Treasury，FRBNY ，Federal Reserve ，2026.9.9

風險偏好升溫與債券供給增加，推升期限溢價與長債殖利率
• 全球期限溢酬上升，長債面臨重新定價：全球債券殖利率同步走高，不僅反映各國政策利率預期，也受到全球期限溢酬上升
推動；投資人對持有長天期政府債券要求更高補償 ，顯示長債殖利率上行已具有跨市場的結構性因素 。Bloomberg
Economics的模型亦顯示，全球期限溢酬是近年G7長債殖利率走高的重要共同因素。
• 風險資產吸引資金，放大長債供需壓力：AI投資題材推升股票市場、信用利差仍相對偏窄，加上大宗商品走強，使股票、公
司債及商品與政府債券形成資金競爭；在政府融資需求持續增加的情況下 ，若債券需求未同步擴張，政府公債需提供更高殖
利率才能吸引資金。
• 後市觀察：長端利率已成為股債配置的重要變數 ，若殖利率因財政供給與風險偏好維持高檔 ，長天期債券及高估值股票均可

能承壓；建議以短中天期美債及高品質投資級公司債為核心 ，待長端殖利率充分反映期限溢酬後，再分批拉長債券存續期間。
資料來源：Bloomberg Economics，2026.9.9

日本央行升息箭在弦上，但缺乏連續升息條件
• GDP修正支持升息 ，但關鍵點在配合日本和美國政府遏制日圓疲軟： 日本第二季 GDP年化季增率上修至 1.4%(優於初值
1.1%)，儘管伊朗戰爭引發原油供應緊張給經濟帶來壓力，但GDP增速依舊高於0.7%潛在經濟成長率，雖然經濟表現支持日
本央行(BOJ)逐步實施貨幣政策正常化 ，但更關鍵的催化劑來自日美兩國協調干預匯市 ，遏制日圓極端貶值的政治壓力 ，彭
博經濟研究預期日本央行將在9月會議上加息25個基點。
• 缺乏連續大幅加息的支撐力道： (1)經濟衝擊風險：日圓升值效應已對經濟產生緊縮效果 ，彭博預估可能拖累實際 GDP成長
達0.3%。(2)財政負擔過重：若政策利率升至 1.75%以上，將大幅增加政府利息支出 ，擠壓高市內閣的財政刺激空間 。(3)企

業投資放緩：私人資本支出仍顯疲軟(季比下降0.9%)，顯示經濟復甦基礎尚未完全穩固。
• 小結：日本央行在政治、匯率與經濟基本面交集下 ，9月升息已成定局，但考量財政負擔與全球金融穩定後 ，其後續加息步
伐應偏向謹慎與循序漸進 ，密切關注9月日本央行會議後聲明 ，若央行強調循序漸進而非連續緊縮 ，市場波動相對可控；反
之，若釋放過度鷹派訊號，則需防範市場拋售風險。由於市場資金對日圓套利交易依賴度仍高，日圓任何短期內的急速升值，
都極易觸發全球風險資產的連鎖去槓桿效應。

中國8月通膨回升，惟需求端疲弱
• 8月份 CPI及PPI：消費者物 價指 數 (CPI)月增 0.4%(前月月減 0.1%)、年增 0.8%(前月 0.5%)，生 產者物價指數 (PPI) 月增
0.4%(前月月減0.7%)、年增3.8%(前月3.5%)。通膨走揚主要受到能源及通訊設備成本上升影響。
• 觀點：中國自2023年以來，內需市場面臨房地產崩盤 、青年失業率飆升以及公共債務攀升等困境 。今年年初以來，通膨雖
然由負轉正，不過從細項得知，因國際油價飆升、AI硬體設備面臨漲價壓力，本波通膨回升主要是受到供給端影響。外部成
本壓力恐進一步壓抑中國內需消費，中國要在真正意義上走出通縮環境可能還需要更多官方政策發力。彭博預估，在需求未
出現明顯改善的情境下，中國央行需要近一步放寬貨幣政策，預測年底前可能降息10個基點。

AI產業獲利亮眼，但需留意變現能力和資本效率
• AI獲利增速領先大盤，銷售成長成為獲利關鍵：AI板塊在2026年第一與第二季分別實現52.8%與59.7%的高獲利成長，受高
基期影響，未來四季度平均獲利成長預計放緩至 34.4%，但仍大幅優於非 AI板塊12.3%。預計2026年第三季至2027年第二
季，AI平均銷售成長率將達 27.2%，為非AI成分股(5%)5倍以上 。隨著未來利潤率擴張放緩 (預計未來 6個季度平均僅提升
1.66%，低於過去12個季度的3.52%)，持續的銷售成長是維持獲利的關鍵。
• AI供應鏈內部發展並不均衡： 半導體最為強勁，2026Q3至2027Q2預期銷售成長65.8%，獲利大增112.1%且淨利潤率大幅
擴張8.86%，為產業鏈核心引擎。雲端巨頭表現穩健，營收與獲利增速維持約26%，基礎設施軟體與硬體也有15%~20%營

收與獲利成長 。但應用端軟體成長相對乏力 ，未來四季度獲利預期增速僅 4.3%與6.5%，表現低於標普500非AI股平均水準。
• 資本強度創新高 ，但自由現金流承壓： AI資本支出佔營收比重自 2024年Q1的8.9%一路攀升至 2026年Q2的20.1%，預計
2026年下半年將達到23%的高點。資本支出激增導致自由現金流與資本支出之比(FCF/Capex)從2.48倍滑落至0.69倍，自由
現金流利潤率亦從21%降至13.9%。隨著現金逐漸消耗，企業可能需透過債券市場進行發債融資 ，以維持龐大基礎設施投資。
• 小結與投資建議：AI板塊依然是美股獲利驅動力，但隨利潤率提升空間受限、自由現金流受資本支出壓制，投資邏輯已從全
面暴漲轉向注重變現能力與資本效率。投資建議聚焦上游硬體與半導體，建議持續優選具備高定價能力與實質訂單支撐的半
導體與雲端基礎設施龍頭，留意資本支出佔比過高且自由現金流急劇惡化公司，優先選擇現金流充沛、融資成本低企業。
標普500指數盈利成長：AI vs. 非AI
資料來源：Bloomberg，2026.9.7

歐央9月升息一碼，措辭更鷹經濟預測上修，12月或再升息
• 歐洲央行9月升息一碼：歐央將三大政策利率各上調25個基點，存款利率升至
2.5%，反映能源價格受地緣政治因素推升，同時上調2027年和2028年的核心
通膨預測分別上修了 0.1個百分點。與此同時，對於經濟成長前景更為樂觀 ，
上調了今年的GDP成長預測，主要反映了2026年第二季數據優於預期以及下
半年內需表現更為穩健 ，此外，年末成長動能的增強為 2027年創造了更有利
的延續效應，也推動了明年經濟成長預期的上調。
• 投資建議： 此次會後 聲明基調偏鷹 ，強調通膨衝擊預計還將延續 ，市場預期
12月或再升息 ，這將有助銀行業利差 ，進一步支撐營收與獲利表現 ，同時，
企業投資與融資需求回升，加上製造業景氣改善帶動放款成長，均有助於歐洲

銀行業擴大收益來源， 建議投資人可以適度配置，增加投組多元性。

近五年美國8月CPI年增率
燃料成本料推動8月運輸成本上升
資料來源：Bloomberg，2026.9.14
• 美國8月通膨數據：8月美國CPI月增0.4%、年增3.4%，核心CPI月增0.3%，核心服務通膨升溫至0.5%，顯示通膨降溫仍不均衡，
能源價格上漲為主要推手，市場預估8月核心PCE月增約0.3%、年增約3.8%，仍高於Fed 2%目標，搭配強勁就業數據，9月升息
1碼的壓力升高。不過，本次通膨主要來自能源、需求及AI投資帶動，與2021～2022年供給型通膨有所不同。
• 市場反應與展望：CPI公布後，2年期美債殖利率一度上升，惟長天期殖利率反而走低，顯示市場關注焦點並非單純升息，而是

Fed能否維持抗通膨政策可信度。整體而言，升息未必等同Risk-off，若經濟與企業獲利維持韌性，股市仍具支撐。

## Document 086: 261006_daiwa_winway

Source PDF: all report pdf/261006_daiwa_winway.pdf
Report source: line報告備份
Date: 2026-10-06

### Page 1

Summary: Winway reported September revenue of TWD1.25bn (-26.7% MoM; +87.7% 
YoY), brining 3Q26 revenue to a record high of TWD4.56bn (+29.3% QoQ; +152.6% YoY). 
The 3Q26 top-line result beat market consensus by 6.7% but missed Daiwa’s estimate by 
9.1%. Following August’s exceptionally strong top line (TWD1.71bn), we had factored in a 
higher shipment run-rate for September. However, the miss stems primarily from the 
lumpiness of probe-card deliveries (high concentration to a single customer) and customer 
qualification timelines, rather than order cancellations or share loss.

We forecast 4Q26 revenue to track flattish to slightly down QoQ, reflecting lower probe-
card shipment volumes compared to 3Q26, alongside typical year-end pull-in deceleration 
from customers. On profitability, we maintain our view that 2Q26 marked the trough for 
gross margin this year. As the revenue contribution from lower-margin MEMS probe cards 
declines, a more favourable product mix should drive a steady QoQ gross margin recovery, 
which may drive gross margin to reach 40% earlier than market expectations.

Although we remain positive on Winway’s structural growth profile, near-term catalysts 
appear muted. This reflects the revenue lumpiness in probe cards, while additional socket 
capacity will not come online until March 2027—pushing the corresponding revenue 
recognition into 2Q27. We will revisit our financial model upon the company’s 3Q26 results 
release. We currently have a Buy (1) rating on Winway with a 12-month TP of TWD10,000.

Topics  China's consumption-boosting measures lack potency

The Chinese government has formulated its first five -year plan dedicated exclusively to expanding consumption. 
However, a rapid recovery from the real-estate slump and anxiety surrounding employment, particularly among young 
people, which lie at the root of consumers' growing preference for saving money, appears unlikely. Achieving the plan's 
goals is therefore expected to prove difficult.

■ Services consumption is positioned as top priority  
In July, the Chinese government formulated a 
plan to expand consumption during the 15th Five-
Year Plan period. This is the first time China has 
released a five -year plan dedicated exclusively to 
consumption. The plan's overarching goal is to 
increase the s hare of household consumption, 
including consumption of a broad range of 
services, in the overall economy. The aim is to 
transform China's economic growth model. 
China's economy has traditionally relied heavily 
on investment -led growth, and household 
consumption accounts for less than 40% of GDP, 
significantly below the 50 –60% seen in major 
European countries and Japan and the figure of 
almost 70% in the U.S. Meanwhile, with the real -
estate slump and excess production capacity 
becoming increasingly serious problems, the 
traditional investment-oriented growth model has 
reached its limits, making a shift to consumption -
driven growth an urgent priority. 
As a specific numerical target, the plan calls for 
raising total retail sales of consumer goods (goods 
retail + catering industry income) to around 60 trillion yuan by 2030. Achieving this target will require 
consumption to expand at an average annual rate of 3.7% over the five-year period. Unlike the overarching 
goal described above, however, this numerica l target does not include consumption of services other than 
the income of the catering industry.  
The plan presents six "crucial areas" for advancing initiatives to expand consumption: 1) qualitatively 
enhancing services consumption, 2) expanding and upgrading goods consumption, 3) fostering new 
consumption forms, models, and scenarios, 4) raising cons umption capacity, 5) optimizing the overall 
consumer environment, and 6) enhancing platforms and strengthening support mechanisms to boost 
consumption. Regarding services consumption, which is positioned at the top of this list, policies include 
supporting consumption in elderly care (silver economy) and childcare services, developing cultural and 
tourism consumption, promoting consumption in health and sports, and enhancing education and training 
services. At the meeting of the CCP Politburo at the end of July, the need to unlock the potential of services 
consumption was also emphasized, suggesting that the focus of the Chinese government's consumption -
boosting measures is shifting from goods to services consumption. 
■ Services consumption is being affected by keenness to save money 
In conjunction with the formulation of the plan, in June the government began publishing a new indicator 
designed to capture trends in consumption of a broad range of services: total retail sales of consumer goods 
and services. In addition to the income of  the catering industry, which was captured by the previous retail 
sales indicator (total retail sales of consumer goods), this new metric includes consumption of various other 
services, such as communication/information services, tourism/culture, and sports/leisure. A comparison of 
recent trends in the old and new indicators shows that while growth in the old indicator has continued to 
slow since 2024, the new indicator is relatively solid. 
Raising total retail sales of consumer goods to 
around 60 trillion yuan

2 
Overall services consumption is therefore firm in 
comparison to goods consumption, though it still lacks 
the strength to become a driver of overall consumption. 
Looking at the breakdown of services consumption, 
spending patterns have diverged amid the econ omic 
slowdown since 2025, when the gap between the old 
and new indicators widened, as consumers have 
increasingly sought to save money.  Expenditure on 
items for which it is difficult to cut spending, such as 
medical/healthcare, transportation/communicatio n, and 
education, has tended to increase faster than disposable 
income. In contrast, spending on services that can be 
substituted with alternatives within the household, such 
as catering and domestic help, has stagnated. Housing -
related expenses, including  rent, have also remained 
weak against the backdrop of the real -estate slump. 
Furthermore, even tourism consumption, which the 
government has positioned as a pillar of consumption 
expansion, has been affected by economic considerations. Travelers are incre asingly favoring low -cost, 
close-to-home, and short-duration trips, with domestic travel more popular than overseas travel. Per capita 
spending by domestic tourists has also declined.  
■ Lack of measures to overcome structural weakness in consumption makes target difficult to 
achieve 
Among the other crucial areas, 3) fostering new consumption forms, models, and scenarios is expected 
to generate some additional demand through measures such as developing digital consumption by 
integrating artificial intelligence with consumption, promoti ng green (eco -friendly) consumption, and 
developing the "first-launch economy" in line with local conditions. The first -launch economy is a catch -
all term for economic activities involving "firsts," such as companies launching new products and 
introducing new business formats, technologies, and services, and overseas and domestic brands opening 
their first stores. 
However, a full -fledged recovery in consumption appears unlikely. The root causes of consumers' 
preference for saving are the negative wealth effect of the real -estate slump and a soft employment 
environment, particularly for young people. Although cities have introduced measures such as easing 
requirements for home purchases, structural factors, including a decline in the pool of potential home buyers, 
continue to weigh on housing sales. Prices of both new homes and used homes, which tend to better reflect

3 
actual market conditions, have continued to decline, and inventory turnover periods also appear to be 
getting longer. A full-fledged recovery in the housing market is therefore unlikely to happen for a few more 
years. The employment situation also shows little sign of improvement. Looking at unemployment rates by 
age group since the beginning of this year, rates for those aged 16–24 and 30–59 have remained at the high 
levels seen before 2025, while the rate for those aged 25 –29 has deteriorated to above las t year's level. 
Consumer confidence, which has been in "pessimistic" territory since 2022 amid the real -estate downturn, 
has worsened further since the start of this year. 
The five-year plan mentions such measures as promoting high -quality and full employment, optimizing 
the supply of affordable housing, and increasing asset income via various channels, such as by improving 
the health of the real-estate market. However, it does not set out specific policies. In addition, advances in 
artificial intelligence could lead to job displacement, causing a further heightening of household anxiety 
about the future, so paradoxically, the technology could actually worsen consumer confidence. Because the 
five-year plan does not adequately address the root causes of weak consumption, the transition to a 
consumption-driven economy is expected to prove challenging. 
  (Hiromu Komiya)

Topics  AI boom is stoking inflation concerns in South Korea

Rising semiconductor prices are spilling over into durable goods prices in South Korea. In addition, widening demands 
for "N% performance-based bonuses," which originated in the semiconductor industry and involve a certain percentage 
of the company's profit being returned to employees in the form of bonuses, could lead to broader-based price increases.

■ Semiconductor prices soar amid the AI boom  
Against the backdrop of the worldwide AI boom, 
prices of memory chips, in which South Korean 
companies hold a large share of the global market, 
have surged. Price increases are spreading beyond 
advanced memory chips for AI data centers to 
general-purpose memory chips used in a wide range 
of digital devices, including PCs and smartphones. 
Semiconductor manufacturers have shifted 
production capacity toward more profitable 
advanced products, resulting in shortages of general-
purpose products and pushing up their prices. 
Such supply -demand pressures are likely to 
persist for the time being. In South Korea's 
semiconductor industry, fabs are operating at high 
utilization rates, leaving limited scope for increasing 
output at existing facilities. Meeting the brisk 
demand thus  necessitates a build -out of new 
production capacity. In response, major South 
Korean chipmakers Samsung Electronics and SK 
hynix are planning large-scale investments, but a substantial expansion in supply is not expected until 2027 
at the earliest. Building large-scale fabs and putting them into operation takes considerable time, and even 
after a facility begins operating, it typic ally takes several months to a year to complete adjustments to 
improve yields and establish mass-production capabilities. There will therefore be a significant wait before 
increased production capacity leads to price stabilization. 
■ Wave of price increases spreads to semiconductor-containing products 
The surge in chip prices has already spilled over 
into prices of finished goods. Price increases have 
been particularly noticeable with products that 
contain large numbers of memory chips, such as 
PCs, smartphones, game consoles, and storage 
devices. Prices of these durable goods had generally 
been on a downward trend over the past 20 years, 
reflecting improvements in performance driven by 
technological advances as well as declining 
production costs. Recently, however, they have 
turned upward, pushing up co nsumer prices. PC 
prices, in particular, have risen markedly, increasing 
by approximately 20% between January and July 
this year.   
Going forward, inflationary pressure is expected 
to remain strong, particularly for durable goods. In 
the case of products containing large quantities of 
semiconductors, manufacturers are likely to 
continue passing higher memory chip costs through 
to selling prices. Furthermore, such developments are not limited to digital devices. The wave of price 
increases could spread to automobiles and smart home appliances, which also contain numerous chips. In 
the auto industry, in particular, the increasing sophistication of vehicles has led to greater semiconductor 
usage, making it likely that soaring manufacturing costs will be passed on to consumers.

■ Demands for "N% performance-based bonuses" are spreading across South Korea 
At South Korean semiconductor giant 
SK hynix, employees received 
exceptionally large performance -based 
bonuses in February this year, with the 
payouts exceeding their annual salaries. 
This was attributable not only to a sharp 
increase in the company's ear nings, 
which were boosted by the global AI 
boom, but also to a revamp last year of 
the firm's bonus system, whereby 10% of 
operating profit would be allocated to a 
pool for performance -based bonuses. In 
May, following SK hynix's enormous 
bonus payout, the labor union at Samsung 
Electronics threatened a strike unless 
employees received performance -based 
bonuses on a similar scale. Although the 
strike was ultimately avoided, labor and 
management in the semiconductor 
division agreed to allocate 10.5% of the 
business's profit to special bonuses. 
Estimates based on this agreement and 
earnings forecasts for this year indicate 
that the average performance -based 
bonus to be paid next year by the 
semiconductor divisions of SK hynix and Samsung Electronics will exceed 50 million yen per employee.   
Demands for "N% performance -based bonuses," whereby a certain percentage of profit is returned to 
employees, are spreading beyond the semiconductor industry into other sectors, too. In manufacturing, 
labor unions in the automobile, heavy industry, non-ferrous metals, and pharmaceutical sectors are calling 
for a certain percentage of operating or net profit to be allocated to performance -based bonuses, and in the 
service sector, unions in segments such as information and communications are making similar dem ands. 
At major IT company Kakao, for example, the labor union launched a strike after demanding that 13 –15% 
of operating profit be allocated to a special bonus pool. Later, following 18 rounds of wage negotiations, 
the union and management reached an agree ment entailing a substantial increase in annual salaries. 
Meanwhile, the labor union at Naver, another IT firm, secured a significant hike in base pay relatively 
quickly by refraining from demanding N% performance-based bonuses. 
■ Rising labor costs could lead to higher prices 
If such trends spread, they could push up wage levels across a broad range of industries, rather than 
simply resulting in temporary increases in performance -based bonuses at highly profitable companies. 
While firms remain cautious about introducing N% perf ormance-based bonuses, labor unions are 
leveraging calls for such schemes in wage 
negotiations, and as noted above, there have already 
been cases in which negotiations surrounding 
performance-based bonuses have resulted in increases 
in base pay. If the high levels of compensation paid in 
the semiconductor and IT industries are emulated by 
other sectors, upward pressure on wages could spread 
more broadly. This is particularly likely in industries 
where competition among companies to attract talent 
is intense, as the firms are conscious of wage increases 
by other companies and tend to follow suit to stay 
competitive in the job market. If such wage 
competition continues, wage hikes at highly profitable 
companies could lead to similar moves at firms whose 
earnings environment is far less favorable.

There is a danger of widespread wage rises intensifying inflationary pressure by pushing service prices 
higher. Wage increases that are not accompanied by productivity gains squeeze corporate earnings as they 
push up labor costs, which creates an incentive for com panies to raise selling prices. This is particularly 
significant in South Korea's service sector, where labor productivity growth has remained sluggish for many 
years, leaving companies with limited scope to absorb wage increases through productivity gains . Indeed, 
in previous periods of rising wages in the service sector, service price inflation has tended to accelerate, so 
the situation will need to be watched closely. 
(Thoma Imagawa)

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
BULLETIN
The Cotality home value index, covering all of Australia,
fell 1.1% in September with downward revisions to the
previous two months again shaving a further 0.4% off prices.
The correction has now seen prices decline 5.2% from their

March peak. Annual growth is flat.
The price correction remains broad, Cotality reporting 97% of
capital city suburbs experienced falls over the three months
to September, Darwin the only city to record gains. Sydney
and Melbourne continue to see more pronounced declines,
albeit moderating a touch, particularly in Melbourne.
Against this, declines in Brisbane, Adelaide and Perth have
accelerated over the last few months. Across segments, unit
prices and lower price tiers continue to track slightly milder

falls than houses and top tiers.
Seasonal variations accentuated price weakness through winter
but are now neutral. The peak-to-trough price decline nationally
is slightly milder at 4.6% in seasonally adjusted terms. Unless
specified, figures in titles, tables and the commentary below are
in non-seasonally adjusted terms, consistent with the ‘headline’
figures reported by Cotality. However, figures shown in charts
are in seasonally adjusted terms.

Preliminary estimates of turnover show rolling 3mth volumes
down 3.9%qtr in September and 17.4%yr with previous
estimates revised 1-2ppts lower. The monthly pattern
suggests small gains in July and August have given way to
another sizeable fall in September.
Looking at the price performance across different jurisdictions:
• Sydney prices dropped 1.4% fall in September, monthly
declines averaging 1.6% over the previous four months,
tracking a 17% annualised pace of decline since May. All

segments and tiers recorded falls in Q3 but with top and
middle tier houses notably weaker.
• Melbourne prices fell 0.7%mth, a slower pace than other
capitals. This likely reflects the weaker starting point coming
into the correction, prices already back to the levels seen in
mid-2021. As elsewhere, 'top tier' houses prices are leading
the decline. 'Low tier' unit prices mostly held their ground in
Q3, prices in this sub-segment already plumbing 10yr lows.

• Brisbane was the weakest capital city price-wise, recording
a 1.5% drop in September with previous monthly declines
also revised down heavily (August from 1% to 1.6% and
July from 1.2% to 1.7%). The combined effect has taken
the rolling 3mth change to –4.7%qtr from –2.7%qtr last
month and –0.6%qtr in July. At 5.9%yr, annual growth is
still positive but losing altitude quickly, having run at 18%yr
as recently as May. Sellers have been slow to respond to

the sudden stop with total listings up over 50% vs a year

(reflecting the extremely tight starting point for the Brisbane

• Adelaide dwelling prices fell 1.3%mth, the August decline

also marked down from 0.8% to 1.1%, annual price growth
dropping to 6.5%yr. The price correction began later in
Adelaide but is now running at a similar pace to prices
nationally.
• Perth recorded a 1.2% decline but also saw big downward
revisions to previous months (August from 0.8% to –1.7%
and July from –1.3% to –1.8%). At 10.1%yr, annual growth
is still solidly positive but was tracking at over 25%yr earlier
in the year. As with Adelaide and Brisbane, the detail shows

a more even spread of declines across price tiers. However,
turnover is showing a milder pull-back in Adelaide.
• The smaller capitals were again more mixed, price growth
still positive in Darwin (0.4%mth, 11.9%yr), relatively
resilient in Hobart (–0.5%mth, 7%yr) but weaker in
Canberra (–1.1%mth, –1.6%yr).
• Regional markets showed a 0.9% decline in Qld but slightly
milder declines in NSW (–0.7%) and Victoria (0.3%). Annual
prices growth is in the 3.4-6.3%yr range.

Our recently released Housing Pulse report (here) provides
a deeper diver into the situation in Australia's housing
markets. While the absence of selling pressure, still tight
supply-demand balance and sentiment mix suggests the
correction was still relatively 'orderly' through the September
quarter, the latest monthly data shows momentum remains
unambiguously negative.
That said, there are clearly more headwinds to come. The
RBA latest interest rate rise and what now looks to be a

more likely prospect of a follow-up increase at its meeting in
early November will impact in coming months. Next week's
consumer sentiment update will show how the change, and
the latest round of weak updates, is shaping housing-related
sentiment. Either way, momentum looks set to remain weak
into year-end and, barring a significantly improved inflation
and interest rate outlook, early 2027.

## Document 239: Europe’s construction sector faces a period of weak growth

Source PDF: all report pdf/Europe’s construction sector faces a period of weak growth.pdf
Report source: ING Think
Date: 2026-10-05
Pages: 11
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

Europe’s construction volumes will keep growing but remain
subdued. Infrastructure investment and Germany’s gradual
rebound are drivers, while higher ﬁnancing costs and
geopolitical risks cap momentum. Housing permits have
stabilised, signalling that a stronger residential recovery will
have to wait
Maurice van Sante
Europe's construction sector is showing signs of stabilising, but growth remains
modest
Low growth returns in 2026
We expect EU construction output to grow by a marginal 0.5% in

2026, following a 1.8% decline in 2024 and 2.3% growth in 2025.
More broadly, EU GDP has outpaced construction output in recent
years, showing that the construction sector has lagged the broader
economy.
The positive signals seen in early 2025 have since weakened. By
September, the EU construction conﬁdence indicator had stopped
improving and had fallen below its level a year earlier. Nevertheless,

EU construction lags GDP growth

Source: Oxford economics, ING Research
The building sector has declined, while infrastructure has grown
The building sector, comprising residential and non-residential

construction, remains a weak spot. Higher interest rates and
construction costs, combined with economic uncertainty, are
making households and businesses more cautious about investing
in new property. By contrast, some segments stand out more
positively. Specialised construction has beneﬁted from renovation
and sustainability projects, while infrastructure investment,
supported by EU funds and spending on energy and digital
networks, has provided an additional oﬀset.

### Page 3

Source: Eurostat, *forecasts ING Research
Following ﬁve consecutive years of contraction, Germany’s
construction sector is expected to grow again in 2026. Europe’s
largest construction market shrank by more than 10% between
2020 and 2025. We expect a modest recovery this year, with
growth gradually gaining momentum in 2027, supported by
housing and infrastructure.
In 2025, France’s construction output fell by 3.5%, followed by
quarter-on-quarter declines of 1.2% and 0.9% in the ﬁrst two

quarters of 2026. French sector conﬁdence weakened further, while
housing permits in August remained below their level a year earlier.
Combined with sluggish GDP growth, this points to a 2.5%
contraction in 2026.
Spain’s construction sector is still recovering. After losing more than
25% of its output between 2019 and 2022, the market began to
rebound in 2023. We forecast growth of 3% in 2026. Building
permits have risen sharply in recent years, although momentum is

easing. Contractor conﬁdence is easing from a very high level in
recent months. Even so, robust GDP growth should support
construction this year and next.
For the Netherlands, we expect construction output to stabilise in
2026 after a weak ﬁrst half, followed by modest growth in 2027.

### Page 4

Housing construction should gradually recover as the recent rise in
building permits feeds through into starts and completions,
although projects are often delayed and the recovery will remain
limited. Infrastructure remains an important driver.
Poland’s construction sector had a volatile start to 2026, with
output falling by 6.8% in the ﬁrst quarter due to the cold winter
before rebounding by 8% in the second. Building permits are rising
slightly, conﬁdence remains stable and robust GDP growth of 3.4%

should support demand. The resumption of EU funding is also
expected to support infrastructure investment. Nevertheless, we
expect construction output to remain ﬂat in 2026, largely because
of the weak ﬁrst quarter, followed by modest growth of 2% in 2027.
Input prices are marginally increasing
Higher energy prices are pushing up production costs for energy-
intensive materials such as concrete, cement and bricks. Even so,
material prices are generally rising only gradually. In September,

13% of EU producers planned to raise selling prices over the next
three months, compared with 11% in January, before the Iran war.
Passing higher input costs through the value chain is also
becoming harder. Governments, non-residential investors and
homebuyers already face higher interest rates, making further cost
increases more diﬃcult to absorb.
Contract terms may also pose a risk for contractors. Public price-
adjustment schemes are often too limited to absorb shocks, while

countries without them, such as Spain, face greater exposure.
Private contracts oﬀer even less protection, increasing the risk of
disputes, delays and halted projects.

### Page 5

Source: Eurostat, ING Research
Low water levels are delaying deliveries
Low water levels are creating upward cost pressure on building
materials. Many raw materials used in building materials, such as
sand, gravel and chemicals, are transported by inland waterways.
When river levels are low, barges can carry less cargo, which
increases transport costs and delays deliveries in the construction
supply chain. The impact has remained limited but is slowly

increasing. In September, 8% of EU construction companies
reported production constraints due to material shortages. This is
above the 5.7% in January and the 6.4% recorded during the low-
water period in September 2018.
Proﬁt conﬁdence rebounds, but energy risks return
Higher energy prices and the prospect of rising building-material
costs initially alarmed construction companies, which feared a
sharp hit to proﬁt margins in the ﬁrst quarter of 2026. As the Iran

war continued, however, gas prices rose less dramatically than
during the 2022 energy crisis. Conﬁdence recovered, and second-
quarter proﬁt expectations exceeded pre-war levels. This rebound
may prove excessive, as energy prices have since begun rising
again.

### Page 6

Source: RICS, ING Research
Residential growth stalls
Between summer 2024 and the end of 2025, the number of
building permits issued in the EU rose by approximately 15%. Since
then, however, permit issuance has broadly stabilised, suggesting
that momentum has faded in recent months. Several factors may
explain this development:
Higher interest rates make homes less aﬀordable, particularly
new builds, because buyers often have to ﬁnance payments
during construction.

The conﬂict in the Middle East has heightened geopolitical
uncertainty. This may make buyers more reluctant to purchase
a home, especially a new build, because completion can take
up to two years. The longer waiting period increases
uncertainty compared with buying an existing home.
Higher energy prices have increased the cost of energy-
intensive building materials, although the rise in overall
construction costs has so far remained modest.
One possible oﬀset is that buyers may prefer new homes because

they are generally more energy-eﬃcient than existing properties.
However, the stabilisation in permit issuance suggests that this
advantage is not strong enough to drive further growth.
The number of permits issued for new houses is an important
indicator of future volumes in the residential sector. Typically, it
takes between one and two years from the granting of a permit to
the completion of a new home.

### Page 7

Recovery of issued building permits slows
EU Building permits new dwellings, SA (index 2021=100
Germany follows the broader European recovery after a particularly
sharp fall in housing permits in 2021 and 2022. The government’s
‘housing construction turbo’ (Bau-Turbo) aims to speed up
planning and approvals in high-demand areas. Its impact, however,
will depend on local authorities, which may hesitate over loss of
control and legal uncertainty.
The permit data for France at the beginning of the year looked

promising. However, the second quarter was disappointing. France
expanded its zero-interest mortgage scheme (PTZ: Prêt à Taux Zéro)
for new-build homes in April 2025 to support ﬁrst-time buyers and
boost housing construction, but the building permit data suggests
the policy has yet to gain traction.
Dutch permits for new homes surged in the second quarter of 2026.
Statistics Netherlands has since paused publication of recent data,
saying the increase appears implausibly large and may reﬂect

legislative changes related to grid congestion. Limited grid capacity
continues to delay new connections and construction projects,
while nitrogen restrictions cause further setbacks. The
government's plan to reduce nitrogen emissions of peak polluters
and make way for other business activities is now under
parliamentary review.
Spanish housing permits nearly tripled between 2020 and 2024,
largely reﬂecting a rebound from the exceptionally low pre-

### Page 8

pandemic era base and a recovery in housing demand. Growth now
appears to have peaked, with permits falling by almost 2% in the
second quarter of 2026. The government’s PERTE programme, co-
ﬁnanced through European recovery funds, aims to speed up and
reduce the cost of housing construction through prefabrication and
modular methods, but its impact will take time.
Business case still diﬃcult
Housing shortages remain severe in many European cities, but new

housing projects are more diﬃcult to make commercially viable in
some countries than in others. Although permit issuance has
increased in many markets, construction costs remain high.
Builders and developers often cannot fully pass these costs on to
buyers, especially where existing homes are still cheaper than a few
years ago. As buyers can choose between new and existing homes,
relatively higher new-build prices are likely to push many towards
existing properties.

This is particularly true in France, Germany and Austria, where
existing-home prices have risen less than the European average. In
France, they remain even below their levels three years ago.
Builders and developers therefore have very limited (or no) scope to
pass on higher material costs, leaving many projects unproﬁtable
at current market prices. As a result, they are postponing
construction until conditions improve.

Infrastructure growth continues
EU infrastructure output grew by 2.2% in 2025. Growth slowed in
the ﬁrst seven months of 2026 to around 0.4% year-on-year. A
relatively cold, wet winter across many European regions
temporarily disrupted activity at infrastructure sites, though the
sector recovered some of the lost ground in the following months.
Highest growth in energy works
Infrastructure subsectors EU-19, Index 2021=100

Source: Euroconstruct, ING Research
The EU energy construction sector has been growing since 2022,
driven by investments in renewable energy and electricity grid
expansion. Growth is expected to remain strong as many countries
in the EU continue to upgrade power infrastructure to support the
energy transition and rising electricity demand.

Download
Railway construction is showing strong growth. Across the EU,
activity is being supported by both network renewals and major

cross-border projects. Examples include Rail Baltica, which will
connect the Baltic States with Poland, the Lyon–Turin rail link
between France and Italy and high-speed rail investment in Spain,
Portugal and Italy. Together, these projects illustrate the EU’s drive
to expand rail capacity.
Telecommunications construction has grown modestly in recent
years, supported by ﬁbre-optic expansion and continued
investment in 5G networks across the EU. Although coverage is

improving, progress varies widely between member states and
signiﬁcant investment is still needed to extend high-capacity
networks, particularly in rural and other lagging areas.
Roadworks, which account for around 30% of EU infrastructure
output, remain under pressure as many local and regional
authorities face budget constraints. Across the EU, public funding is
competing with other investment and spending priorities, limiting
the resources available for road construction and maintenance.

Overall, Europe’s construction recovery will remain slow and uneven.
Infrastructure investment and a gradual German recovery will
provide support, but high ﬁnancing costs, weak housing economics
and geopolitical risks will limit momentum. For now, infrastructure
will do most of the heavy lifting within the sector.

## Document 245: Financial conditions in the Eurozone_ a tightening temporarily tempered by credit volumes (2026-10-07)

Source PDF: all report pdf/Financial conditions in the Eurozone_ a tightening temporarily tempered by credit volumes (2026-10-07).pdf
Report source: BNP Paribas
Date: 2026-10-07
Pages: 3
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

7 October 2026
The bank
for a changing
world
FINANCIAL CONDITIONS IN THE EUROZONE: A TIGHTENING TEMPORARILY TEMPERED BY CREDIT VOLUMES
Thomas Humblot
*LATEST OBSERVATION: 5 OCTOBER 2026  **ESTIMATE
Since June 2026, the ECB has been publishing 1 a new indicator that helps to assess the transmission of its monetary policy to the economy:
the Broad Intermediation Gauge 2 (ECB-BIG). This new tool has revealed that, thus far, strong credit growth has limited the restrictive impact

of tightening financial conditions caused by rising interest rates in the Eurozone. However, this offsetting effect is beginning to fade.
The ECB-BIG: a new indicator for a broader view of financial conditions
The ECB-BIG complements the range of indicators used by the European Central Bank (ECB) to assess the effective transmission of its monetary
policy. This new tool provides a comprehensive view of how restrictive (or expansive) financial conditions are in the Eurozone. It stands out

for its approach that focuses more specifically on intermediation conditions. Therefore, the ECB-BIG provides a more comprehensive view than
most other financial condition indicators of financial conditions. In fact, it covers variables relating to the volume of bank lending to households
and businesses (the ‘volume’ component). Traditionally, this type of indicator tends to focus more on market variables (primarily interest and
exchange rates), share prices and corporate spreads. In addition, the ‘price’ component of the ECB-BIG is extended to include the cost of bank

loans, which is another new feature.
The rise in interest rates was temporarily offset by increased lending
By aggregating the variables relating to banking and non-banking intermediation conditions, the ECB-BIG shows that rising interest rates
(negative price effect) were offset in particular by lending volumes, which have continued to rise in 2026 (positive volume effect). A more
‘traditional’ indicator of financial conditions, the Macro-Finance FCI (MF-FCI) — which is, incidentally, included in the ECB-BIG — reveals a

much more pronounced tightening over the same period (see the orange curve in the chart). However, this offsetting of the price effect by the
volume effect is now beginning to wane, a sign that monetary policy is being effectively transmitted to the economy. Financial intermediaries
are gradually passing on the rise in rates to their customers (see the dotted green lines, which represent the composite indicators of the cost
of new loans to households and businesses, combining interest rates for different maturities), while lending volumes are falling. As a result,

between July and August 2026, seasonally adjusted flows of loans to households and businesses almost halved.
1 P . Lane, 2026, The Outlook for the Euro Area Economy, Background slides for Reuters NEXT Europe, London, 16 June 2026
2 A. Allayioti et al., 2026, The ECB-BIG index: tracking credit conditions in the euro area, ECB Economic Bulletin, Issue 5/2026
SOURCE: ALLAYIOTI AND AL. (2026), ECB, BLETZINGER ET AL. (2026), BNP PARIBAS ESTIMATES

In the longer term, financial conditions are expected to tighten, including as measured by the ECB-BIG
Despite the very sharp widening of sovereign spreads between 28 September and 2 October, financial conditions as measured by the
MF-FCI did not tighten, with sovereign yields actually falling over this period (with the exception of the OAT and the BTP), for example.

Nevertheless, the high volatility of the bond market, particularly in the sovereign segment, is likely to continue to affect financial
conditions. Looking slightly further ahead, the ECB’s key interest rate rises — the one that occurred on 16 September and another that we
anticipate for December 2026 (both +25 bp) — are likely to result in a more pronounced tightening. With a time lag of a number of weeks,
financial conditions, as defined by the ECB-BIG, are also expected to tighten. The price effect is likely to lead to a negative volume effect

on this occasion.

## Document 275: Is FEMO Too Concentrated_

Source PDF: all report pdf/Is FEMO Too Concentrated_.pdf
Report source: researchmail轉寄
Date: 2026-10-14
Pages: 10
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

From:    Yardeni QuickTakes <yardeni-research@ghost.io>
Subject: Is FEMO Too Concentrated?
Date:    29 minutes ago
Is FEMO Too Concentrated?

YARDENI QUICKTAKES
Is FEMO Too Concentrated?
By Ed Yardeni & Elias Griepentrog • 13 Oct 2026
View in browser
Photo by Michal Balog / Unsplash

I. Stocks: Happy Fourth Anniversary!
Yesterday marked the fourth anniversary of the stock market bull run. The
S&P 500 is up 118.3% since it began. The bull market has survived the
pandemic, global supply-chain disruptions, rebounding inflation, two oil
price shocks, a significant round of Fed rate hikes, soaring bond yields, a
financial crisis, Trump's tariffs, and two wars. It has also survived concerns
about the market’s leadership being too concentrated in a few
outperforming stocks.

### Page 2

Only two of the S&P 500's 11 sectors have outperformed the index:
Information Technology (258.1%) and Communication Services (196.7%).
The others posted reasonably strong bull-market returns. Keep in mind
that the three most underperforming sectors include many companies that
pay relatively high dividends to shareholders (charts).

### Page 3

Since 2025, the current bull market has been increasingly led by FEMO
(Fabulous Earnings Momentum). Since the bull market started, S&P 500
forward earnings is up 73.9%, while the forward P/E is up 25.4% (chart).
A recent concern is that FEMO has been too concentrated. Our diffusion
indexes show that the percentages of S&P 500 companies with positive 3-
month and 12-month increases in forward revenues and forward earnings
have risen significantly during the bull market (charts).

### Page 4

On a y/y basis, all sectors are showing solid gains in forward earnings,
with the three worst laggards including many companies that pay relatively
high dividends to shareholders, as noted above (chart).

### Page 5

Nevertheless, this has been a tough year for stock pickers so far, with
41.2% of S&P 500 companies’ stocks down ytd (chart).
II. Update on the US Economy
As we noted recently, analysts expect positive revenue and earnings
growth across all 11 S&P 500 sectors in Q3 on a y/y basis. This broad-
based strength reflects the remarkable resilience of the US economy. The
latest economic indicators provide further evidence of that resilience.
Here's more:

(1) Bank earnings. The big banks kicked off Q3 earnings season today,
with JPMorgan Chase, Goldman Sachs, Citigroup, and Wells Fargo
reporting solid results. Their commentary confirms that strong consumer
spending and business investment are translating to robust lending growth

### Page 6

and investment banking activity, while credit quality remains healthy
(chart).
JPMorgan CEO Jamie Dimon pointed to "strong business investment and
ongoing consumer spending," while Goldman Sachs CEO David Solomon
observed that "the economy continues to be very, very resilient despite
some of the headwinds." Wells Fargo CEO Charlie Scharf offered a
particularly upbeat assessment, noting that despite mixed consumer
sentiment, "consumers are spending more, loan delinquencies are low,

and deposit and investment balances have grown." He added that strong
employment, wage growth, and AI infrastructure investment continue to
outweigh the risks from higher interest rates, oil prices, and inflation.
Citigroup CEO Jane Fraser likewise observed that despite all the attention
surrounding the AI boom, "the consumer is still driving much of the
growth."
(2) Consumer spending. The latest Redbook data confirm the bank
CEOs’ upbeat assessments of the US consumer. Redbook same-store

sales surged 10.4% y/y during the week of October 9, the strongest
increase since 2022 (chart).

### Page 7

Bank of America's September Consumer Checkpoint provides further
evidence of consumer strength. Credit and debit card spending per
household rose 6.2% y/y, or 5.1% excluding gasoline, both the third-
fastest rates in four years! Even more encouraging, spending growth is
broadening across income groups. Card spending rose 5.5% y/y among
lower-income households, compared with 4.9% and 4.8% among middle-
and higher-income households, respectively. Only the top 5% of earners

posted stronger growth, at 5.9%.
(3) Labor market. What's behind the broadening of consumer spending?
A key pillar is the labor market. Bank of America also reports that after-tax
wage and salary growth reached 4.5% y/y among lower-income
households in September, compared with 3.8% and 3.7% among middle-
and higher-income households, respectively. Stronger wage growth is
helping consumers absorb higher living costs without sacrificing
discretionary spending.

The latest weekly ADP data suggest that labor market strength persists.
Private-sector employment rose by 24,750 during the week of September
26, the sixth consecutive weekly acceleration and the strongest gain since
early June (chart). That implies a monthly pace of roughly 96,300 jobs,
comfortably above most estimates of the breakeven pace needed to keep
the unemployment rate low and stable.

### Page 8

Meanwhile, small business hiring plans moderated somewhat in
September but remained above historical norms. The latest NFIB survey
showed that 17% of small businesses planned to increase employment,
well above the historical average of 11% (chart). Job openings remained 8
percentage points above their historical average. A shortage of qualified
workers continues to constrain hiring, with 45% of firms reporting no
qualified applicants for their open positions compared with the historical

average of 37%.
The bad news is that the NFIB survey shows that pricing pressures
remained elevated in September (chart).

### Page 9

The most frequently cited problem faced by small business owners is the
quality of labor (chart). We expect they will solve this problem by boosting
productivity through AI and other technologies.
💡

log in to the Yardeni QuickTakes website and post them at the end
of the QuickTakes article. Paid members' contributions may be
featured in our segment, "Ed Answers Your Questions".

## Document 336: The U.S. Corporate Tax Base That Now Resides in Ireland (2026-09-03)

Source PDF: all report pdf/The U.S. Corporate Tax Base That Now Resides in Ireland (2026-09-03).pdf
Report source: CFR (Follow the Money)
Date: 2026-09-03
Pages: 17
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

1
EXPERT TAKE FOLLOW THE MONEY
The U.S. Corporate Tax Base That Now Resides in
Ireland
America’s largest companies often pay more tax in Dublin than they do in D.C.
A statue of former Sen. Albert Gallatin stands at the Treasury Department in Washington, U.S., April 25, 2021. REUTERS/Al Drago
PUBLISHED
Articles Follow the Money Share
Log In
Regions Topics Research Experts Events The Future of American Strategy

### Page 2

2
September 3, 2026 6:43 p.m.
Brad W. Setser CFR EXPERT
Whitney Shepardson Senior Fellow
Michael Weilandt
Research Associate, Geoeconomics
This is a joint post with Michael Weilandt, a
Research Associate at the Council on Foreign
Relations.
Foreign multinationals, mostly American
multinationals, booked just under $300 billion in
profits in Ireland last year.
That isn’t just because Ireland used to have a 12.5
percent headline corporate tax rate, and its current 15

percent rate on large firms is below the U.S. headline
rate of 21 percent.

### Page 3

3
Ireland also permits deductions for qualifying
expenditure on intangible assets, including the
purchase of intra-group intellectual property, which
can substantially reduce firms effective Irish tax rate.*
And since Ireland is a member of the OECD and
most firms now want to book their profits in “BEPS”
compliant jurisdictions, Ireland is the center for
corporate tax avoidance that isn’t (quite) a pure tax
haven.
The names of the firms that take the most advantage

of lower tax rates in Ireland aren’t exactly a secret.
Apple—widely thought to be Ireland’s largest
individual taxpayer thanks to its mostly Irish $80
billion “global” profit—hasn’t reported the amount of
cash it paid the Irish government in 2025.
Microsoft has reported the tax it pays the Irish
government. Per its latest 10-K (p.75), in FY 2026, it
booked over 80 percent of its $62.5 billion
international profit in Ireland. Microsoft also reports

that it actually paid $6.5 billion to the Irish
government (and another $5.5 billion in tax in other
foreign jurisdictions), more than the $6.25 billion it
paid to the U.S. Treasury. Paid here means a transfer
to the Irish treasury, not a set aside to cover expected
FY 2026 taxes.** It set aside $12 billion to cover
expected 2026 foreign tax payments, and $2.5 billion
for expected U.S. federal income tax.

### Page 4

4
Eli Lilly, like Microsoft, paid more in tax to Dublin
than to D.C. Eli Lilly’s 10-K states that it paid $6.6
billion in tax to Ireland in 2025, and only $3.3 billion
to the U.S.
Now, Eli Lilly likely also played a bit of a tax game in
(calendar year) 2025, as its Irish tax payments were
unusually high.
Lilly and the other big pharmaceutical companies
shipped to the United States back in the first quarter
of 2025. Back then the pharmaceutical companies

were petrified that President Trump might actually
impose real tariffs on profitable American firms
playing tax games abroad. Those tariffs though never
materialized. Firms did “deals” with the Trump
administration instead.
But the threat of tariffs had a huge impact on the
trade data—Lilly rushed to produce Mounjaro’s
active ingredient in its Irish factories, and it then
rushed the product to the U.S. ahead of the tariff
deadline (there is a six-digit line item in the trade

data that is more or less Mounjaro, for the curious).
And it seems like Lilly’s Irish subsidiary booked the
profit on its sales to Lilly U.S. ahead of any actual sale
to U.S. consumers.***

### Page 5

5
The net result, obviously, was a big Irish tax liability.
And a big windfall for Ireland’s Treasury, as Lilly
displaced Pfizer as Ireland’s third largest corporate tax
revenues.
The $15 billion a year that these firms generally pay
in tax in Ireland is real money, as they say (they paid
€13 billion in 2024 according the Irish fiscal council;
the 2025 number will be out soon).
Ask the top brass at the Pentagon. $15 billion a year
is probably three new attack subs, or five Arleigh

Burke class destroyers (think mobile air defense
systems). Or for several batteries of Patriots (if
Raytheon can make them). Had those funds been
paid to the U.S., it would not quite cover the cost of
an aircraft carrier (costs have increased recently) but
it would have come close. $30 billion over two years is
a carrier task force. Enough, in other words, to make
the tradeoff between guns and butter a bit less acute.

### Page 6

6
That is simply from tax that firms are paying in
Ireland on the profit from intellectual property largely
developed in the United States.
And profit shifting remains a real problem.
That is what the Irish data from 2025 shows us. And
there is no sign that 2026 will be much different.
A lot of that is Apple and Microsoft.
Both have global profits will in excess of the total
profits of all the large American pharmaceutical
companies.
But Apple and Microsoft are essentially moving the

profit from their foreign sales to Ireland. They still
pay a decent amount of U.S. tax (as do Alphabet and
Nvidia, which both U.S.-shored their intellectual
property a few years back).****

### Page 7

7
The tax game of “Big Pharma” (the top six U.S.
pharmaceutical companies by revenue) is a bit
different. Most aggressively try to move the profit on
their U.S. sales out of the U.S., which generally has
required offshoring both their intellectual property
and the actual production of the active ingredients for
their most profitable drugs.
Back in 2023 and 2024, the top six U.S.
pharmaceutical companies didn’t set aside anything
to pay expected U.S. tax. They have pointed out (to

me, and to Senator Wyden) that they did make cash
payments to the U.S. Treasury, but those payments
were the settlement of the legacy tax liability on their
pre-2017 profit (don’t ask, it is complicated—the key
words are deferral and deemed repatriation).
In 2025, the major pharmaceutical companies did set
aside a few billion dollars to pay U.S. tax. Eli Lilly
doesn’t seem to be quite as aggressive in sweeping
profits out of the U.S. as its peers; it has consistently

reported setting aside a bit of money to pay expected
U.S. taxes on its global profit. Johnson and Johnson,
which historically has swept all its profit out of the
United States, also had to set aside funds to pay U.S.
tax as part of some litigation related tax gymnastics.
But make no mistake, Big Pharma continues to
engage in aggressive forms of tax avoidance —and
still pays almost nothing in U.S. tax. See the chart
below.

### Page 8

8
And there is no evidence—at least, none that we see
—that suggests any of the Trump deals have changed
the core tax structure of big pharma.*****
The top six generally still report losing money on their
U.S. operations.
The big companies also generally report that the U.S.
accounts for most of their revenue.
And they generally report that they make most of
their money offshore (particularly if “special
situations” like corporate reorganizations, mergers,

and litigation set asides are left out). Just look at the
firms’ own 10-Ks.
AbbVie reports losing $3.5 billion in the U.S. and
making $10 billion abroad.

### Page 9

9
Pfizer reports just $776 million in U.S. profit and
almost $7 billion abroad.
Bristol Myers Squib reported a $19 million loss in the
U.S. and $9.3 billion profit abroad.
Merck reported a $5 billion loss in the U.S. and $26
billion profit abroad.
Johnson and Johnson would be the same in a typical
year. Back in 2024, it reported a $500 million loss in
the U.S. and $17 billion profit abroad, but its 2025
tax liability was driven by the reversal of some

litigation set asides (see page 69 of J&J’s latest 10-K).
Lilly seems to have a more balanced tax structure: in
2025, it reported making $14 billion in the U.S. and
$12 billion abroad.

### Page 10

10
As a result, the leading U.S. pharmaceutical
companies pay more in tax abroad than in the United
States, with several firms setting aside nothing to
cover their expected U.S. income tax liability.
In a normal year, without any corporate
reorganizations or litigation related profits, only one
of the top six would set aside a meaningful sum to pay
U.S. tax. So, in a typical year, there would something
like $100 billion in profits and only a few billion set

aside to pay expected U.S. tax.
This shows up in a couple of easily verifiable plots.

### Page 11

11
While Big Pharma is the most obvious case, the
incentive to offshore jobs, production, and profits isn’t
limited to one sector.
In the technology sector, for example, a number of
firms set aside much less to cover expected U.S.
corporate tax payments than would be expected given
their large global profits.

### Page 12

12
Take a very strategic sector, the production of the
equipment used to manufacture semiconductors.
Applied Materials reports making all its money
abroad (almost certainly in Singapore, as noted in its
10-K, where Applied Materials “have been granted
additional conditional reduced tax rates” which expire
in fiscal 2030) and almost nothing in the U.S.

### Page 13

13
Lam Research also reports making all its income
abroad, using Malaysia as its tax hub (from its 10-K:
“Effective from fiscal year 2022, the Company has a
15-year tax incentive ruling in Malaysia for one of its
foreign subsidiaries.”)

### Page 14

14
KLA is a bit more patriotic; it actually pays a bit of
U.S. tax.
To be sure, none of this is in any way illegal. Every
firm can honestly report that it pays all the tax that it
is legally due.
The issue is the law, which continues to create “pay
tax in America last” incentives, and often “produce in
America last” incentives too.
This can and should be changed, likely through a
bipartisan coalition that unites around a patriotic tax
agenda.
* Most U.S. big pharma firms are still paying a bit of

cash to the U.S. Treasury to settle their deferred
international tax liability from the ere Tax Cuts and
(Irish) Jobs Act; the cash tax paid thus exceeds the
amount they set aside out of current profit for
expected current tax. A few firms have tried to use
this fact to obscure the fact that they don’t think they
owe anything to the U.S. federal government out of
their 2023 or 2024 profits.
** Alphabet and Nvidia benefit from the lower tax
rate on foreign profits in the U.S. tax code; they aren’t

paying tax on their foreign profits at 21 percent. But
because their IP is in the U.S., the U.S. government
gets the lion’s share of their tax paid. When the IP is
offshore, the U.S. only gets a small top up tax.

### Page 15

15
This work represents the views solely of the author(s). The
Council on Foreign Relations is an independent,
nonpartisan membership organization, think tank, and
publisher, and takes no institutional positions on matters
of policy.

ND 4.0) License.

***
**** Large groups with annual consolidated revenues
of at least €750 million are subject to a 15 percent
minimum effective tax rate under Pillar Two.
***** Gilead is much less aggressive in its profit
shifting.

## Document 351: ueh22359

Source PDF: all report pdf/ueh22359.pdf

### Page 1

ab 12 October 2026
Global Research
Largan Precision
Core weakness outweighs progress in AI optics
CPO progress is more tangible but substantial success is already priced in
We maintain Sell and our NT$5,000 target price as erosion in the core smartphone
business outweighs CPO progress. The Q326 call confirmed that Largan's fiber array has
passed customer qualification although its customer qualified another vendor's prism
micro-lens assembly (PMLA) that Largan would now buy in. If Largan qualifies the full

FAU plus carrier assembly for 2H27, content rises but with more pass-through cost and
less margin upside. We roll our valuation to 2028E, the first full year of CPO production,
though 25% below the NT$6,660 close on October 8.
Full FAU assembly would lift content, but with more pass-through
We believe Largan's FA customer is one of two  module suppliers on NVIDIA's 2H27
program, which we estimate could reach 40% share. The program is still subject to

successful yield ramp and could move to metalens in 2028, which would reopen supplier
qualification. Assembling the full FAU could lift Largan's ASP to US$125-150 leveraging
its precision alignment and automation but the PMLA, V-groove and carrier parts would
be pass through that cuts into margin leverage. We expand our FAU TAM analysis
(Figure 9S u m m a r y  o f  F A U  d r i v e r s :  1 2 . 3 m n / 3 5 . 6 m n  b a s e  c a s e  i n  2 0 2 8 - 2 0 3 0 ) with FAU market in 2028/30 expanding to 12.3mn/35.6mn units, with Largan

base case set at 25% of the TAM.
Core smartphone business stays soft into 2027
Q326 sales of NT$15.7bn, +15% QoQ/-11% YoY missedUBSe/Street +36%/+33% on
weaker flagship smartphone demand. GMs fell 7.2ppts QoQ to 42.2%, lowest in 13
years on renewable energy costs and low variable-aperture yields, dropping OpM to 17
year lows at 30.3% and EPS to NT$39.6, below UBS/street NT$51.50/NT$49.69. We cut
Q4 to NT$16.7bn +7% QoQ, 11% below consensus as its customer trimmed orders

after a pricier iPhone 18 launch and possible lag to Genius which is picking up periscope
share and outgrew in Q3 (+1% YoY vs. -11% YoY). We cut 2027 from NT$77bn to NT
$67bn, before a recovery to NT$79bn in 2028 with the next periscope upgrade. We see
GM recovering to 47.5%/48.9% in 2027-28 as yields improve and energy costs pass
through in 2028 pricing.
Maintain Sell and our NT$5,000 target
We cut 2026E/27E EPS by 14%/16% to NT$160/NT$180 and introduce 2028E EPS of

NT$220 for the core smartphone business. We value the lens business at NT$3,300 (15x
2028E ex-CPO EPS of NT$220, the middle of Largan's 13–18x historical range) and CPO
option at NT$1,700 or US$7bn (49x our base-case 2028E CPO EPS of NT$35, near the
top of optical peers' 14–54x). Our base case gives Largan 25% of a 12.3mn-unit, US
$1.85bn FAU market at US$150 per unit and a 50% gross margin. That credits it with
yields and margins it still needs to prove in a quarter when yields and energy costs cut

7.2ppts from its lens margin. At ~NT$6,660, the shares imply ~NT$69 of 2028E CPO
EPS, about twice our base case.

CPO fiber array qualified, but rising pass-through as it upgrades to FAU
production
Largan's fiber array (FA) has passed qualification with a volume customer, and
management expects mass-production capacity by mid-2027 in a newly completed fab
dedicated to FA/FAU, using in-house equipment. The company's next step is to produce
the full detachable FAU mounted on a carrier, a board with three alignment beams that
sits on the optical engine's receptor. Largan can build FAUs now and says the customer

### Page 3

Largan Precision 12 October 2026 ab 3
would prefer it to supply the full assembly, but qualification is pending with the
customer preferring FAU and carrier sampled separately.
Even as its potential to supply the full FAU improve, Largan will buy in most of the
content. The customer has qualified another PMLA vendor and prefers semiconductor-
made optics; V-grooves and carrier beams and boards are outsourced and the customer
does not want Largan to make MT ferrules. Management described its role as closer to a

contractor and said margins hinge on yields, as the FA carries a high ASP but also a large
bill of materials.  Supplying the complete FAU could raise billed content, but a larger
purchased bill of materials also dilutes the relationship between revenue and value
added. Our full-FAU ASP assumption requires Largan to capture that invoice scope.
Next year's product is grating-coupled and works for both CPO and NPO while edge-
coupled designs are being explored with the customer. The current product does not go

directly to end customers, but management expects to work directly with end customers
on next-generation designs (likely 2028) as it progresses toward multi-row FAs.
To ramp production, Largan has bought several land parcels and will build new fabs from
scratch, with construction finishing around 2030. It may outsource some capacity in the
interim and said it cannot yet be sure capacity will meet demand.
Annual FAU TAM reaches US$1.85bn in 2028 and US$4.5bn in 2030 in

our base case
We update our FAU (fiber array unit) TAM factoring CPO/NPO penetration into
AI networking switches, NVIDIA multi-rack systems, scale-up on compute and switch
boards, and non-NVIDIA accelerators.  We assume one passive FAU assembly connecting
a fiber array to a photonic engine with one unit per optical engine.
We assume one FAU per optical engine and 32 per switch ASIC in our base case,
matching NVIDIA's 32x3.2T engines on its 102.4T Spectrum-X package. Broadcom's

102.4Tb/s Davisson uses 16 engines at 6.4Tb/s, so our bear case assumes and even mix
of these two designs (24 per ASIC). We note that higher switch bandwidth does not
automatically increase FAU count if engine bandwidth rises at the same rate. Our 2030
assumption maintains the 32 FAUs per switch based on bandwidth rising to 204.8Tb/s
ASICs using 6.4Tb/s optical engines.

Our base case for the FAU market sums up 4 opportunities for CPO/NPO attach:
1. Scale-out and scale-across switches
We estimate annual switch ASIC shipments x CPO + NPO penetration x optical engines
per ASIC x FAUs per engine. In 2028, 25% CPO/NPO penetration or 1.0m 102.4T switch
ASICs at 32 FAUs each produces 8.0m FAUs. In 2030, 55% of 1.1mn 204.8T switch
ASICs at 32 FAUs drives a market size of 19.4mn FAUs.

2. NVIDIA's multi-rack connections for Rubin Ultra and Feynman
NVIDIA at its 2026 GTC San Jose proposed a multi-rack option to network together 8
Oberon NVL72 racks for the Rubin Ultra generation and 8 Kyber NVL144 racks.

Feedback at the time showed  possible transceiver or NPO connections for Rubin Ultra
and CPO connections for Feynman.  Our 2028 base case uses 110,000 racks, 15% multi-
rack adoption and 75% CPO/NPO penetration, giving approximately 12,400 connected
racks.
For 2030, our rack count falls to 35,000 racks as we assume Kyber would have 4x the
number of GPUs, equivalent to 140k Oberon equivalents. We assume the 35,000 racks
at 55% multi-rack adoption x 80% CPO/NPO attach gives 15,400 connected racks. We

assume about 216 and 576 inter-rack FAUs per rack, respectively, driving a market
opportunity of 2.7mn FAUs in 2028 and 8.9mn in 2030.

Overall market opportunity: 12.3mn/35.6mn generates a US$1.85bn/US
$4.5bn TAM in 2028/2030
We sum four pools: scale-out/scale-across switches, NVIDIA multi-rack pods, CPO native

NVIDIA GPUs and non-NVIDIA scale-up.  We add 10%/5% for FAUs lost at downstream
attach and test and for field spares, driving a market size of in our base case of 12.3mn
FAUs in 2028 and 35.6mn in 2030. At US$150/US$125 per FAU, that is a US$1.85bn
market in 2028 and US$4.5bn in 2030 (bear/bull US$0.5bn/US$3.5bn in 2028; US
$1.4bn/US$9.0bn in 2030). The expansion to 2030 comes from wider optical adoption
and greater content in scale-up systems, despite an assumed FAU ASP decline from US

$150 to US$125.

Scenario analysis on Largan's CPO opportunity
Our base case gives Largan the benefit of the doubt for successfully ramping up a full
FAU production and capturing 25% of the overall FAU TAM, yielding NT$35 of 2028E
EPS, about 16% of our 2028E lens EPS. A 25% share means 3.1mn FAUs in its first full
year of production: about 29% of NVIDIA-linked units and roughly three-quarters of the

allocation our checks suggest its module partner holds. We book these at the full US
$150 FAU price and 50% gross margin, allowing for an early-supply premium even
though the optics, V-grooves and carrier parts are bought in. That gives 2028E CPO sales
of US$462mn, rising to US$1.1bn and NT$84 of EPS in 2030 on a larger market.
Our bear case (15% share, 45% GM) gives NT$5 of CPO EPS in 2028E and NT$14 in
2030E; our bull case (35% share, 55% GM) gives NT$103 and NT$267. Each 5ppt of

share moves 2028E CPO EPS by ~NT$6.9 (~NT$340/share at 49x), and each 5ppt of
gross margin by ~NT$4.6 (~NT$225/share).  If gross margin were 35% rather than 50%,
holding operating expenses at 12% of sales, EPS would fall to approximately NT$21. A
next-generation shift in lens or coupling design could also change content and
alignment requirements including a potential metalens design which could invite in
different suppliers.
Our 2030 base case implies ~8.9mn Largan FAUs a year (21mn in the upside case),

though with risks around competition in a crowded field, yields and technology. A shift
to semiconductor-made optics or metalens would loosen alignment tolerances and
erode the precision premium behind Largan's pitch. The upside lies in direct work with
cloud providers on multi-row FAs from 2028, where Largan's multi-layer alignment is
most differentiated.

Valuation and investment implications
We roll our valuation forward to 2028E and value Largan on a sum of the parts. We

value the lens business at NT$3,300 (15x 2028E EPS, the middle of Largan's 13–18x
historical range) and CPO at NT$1,700 (49x our base-case 2028E CPO EPS of NT$35,
near the top of optical peers' 14–54x). Our base case gives Largan 25% of a 12.3mn-
unit, US$1.85bn FAU market at US$150 per unit and a 50% gross margin. The 49x
multiple also assigns a substantial value to a business whose complete assembly still
requires qualification and whose margins still need to demonstrate can reach similar

levels to its mature smartphone business. At ~NT$6,660 recent share price level, the
shares imply ~NT$69 of 2028E CPO EPS, about twice our base case.
Upside risks include faster full-FAU qualification, firm volume commitments from
additional customers, stronger lens demand and better yields. Near-term catalysts are
the customer's capacity forecast and its visit to the new fab in the coming month. A
large forecast or faster FAU plus carrier qualification would be upside risks to our sell.

Forecast returns
Forecast price appreciation -24.9%
Forecast dividend yield 1.2%
Forecast stock return -23.7%
Market return assumption 6.3%
Forecast excess return -30.0%
Company Description
Founded in 1987, Largan Precision (Largan) is one of the world's leading manufacturers of
lenses used in cameras and IT products. It specialises in plastic/hybrid lenses and, to a lesser
extent, glass lenses and modules. In 2022, 20MP+ camera lenses were 10-20% of Largan's

shipment mix, 10MP lenses were 50-60%, 8MP lenses were 0-10% and other lenses were
30-40%.
Valuation Method and Risk Statement
Our price target is based on SOTP valuation on the CPO opportunity and traditional lens
business.
We think Largan's downside risks include: 1) ongoing pricing pressure from smartphone OEM
clients and increasingly intense competition among lens suppliers; and 2) a slower-than-
expected smartphone demand recovery in China, and 3) CPO commercial success below the

market's elevated expectations.
We believe the key upside risk is a faster-than-expected CPO qualification process at Largan.
In addition, if the company demonstrates exceptionally efficient and highly accurate
automation capabilities, it could support greater market share gains within the industry than
we currently anticipate.

Investing in structured investments involves significant risks. For a detailed discussion of the risks involved in investing in any particular structured investment, you must read

the relevant offering materials for that investment. Structured investments are unsecured obligations of a particular issuer with returns linked to the performance of an
underlying asset. Depending on the terms of the investment, investors could lose all or a substantial portion of their investment based on the performance of the underlying
asset. Investors could also lose their entire investment if the issuer becomes insolvent. UBS does not guarantee in any way the obligations or the financial condition of any

issuer or the accuracy of any financial information provided by any issuer. Structured investments are not traditional investments and investing in a structured investment is
not equivalent to investing directly in the underlying asset. Structured investments may have limited or no liquidity, and investors should be prepared to hold their investment
to maturity. The return of structured investments may be limited by a maximum gain, participation rate or other feature. Structured investments may include call features and,

if a structured investment is called early, investors would not earn any further return and may not be able to reinvest in similar investments with similar terms. Structured
investments include costs and fees which are generally embedded in the price of the investment. The tax treatment of a structured investment may be complex and may differ
from a direct investment in the underlying asset. UBS and its employees do not provide tax advice. Investors should consult their own tax advisor about their own tax situation

before investing in any securities.
